## Phase 15 amendments (Account + Cross-device sync via CloudKit, 2026-05-28)

The round-6 Spencer backlog entry "Login + account system (Google + Apple + email)" graduates after the dad-led architecture conversation Spencer paused on. **Dad picked CloudKit** as the backend strategy after weighing four alternatives (CloudKit / WordPress users / Firebase or Supabase / local-only + manual iCloud sync). The architectural pivot dramatically simplifies what Spencer originally captured: with CloudKit, the user's iCloud account *is* the sync identity — no OAuth providers, no password handling, no login UI at all. There is just a Settings → iCloud Sync toggle on top of the CloudKit private database under the hood. The full 14-CL trail below is bigger than a typical phase amendment because the round-6 backlog entry explicitly required surfacing four constitutional touch points (§1 / §3 / §4 / §9 + CL-5) *before* any code lands, and each touch point gets its own decision + considered-alternatives entry per the Spencer-CL convention.

- **CL-86 (US-41 / T-700, 2026-05-28)** — **Backend strategy decision: CloudKit private database, NOT WordPress users / Firebase-Supabase / local-only + manual iCloud sync.** This is the load-bearing architectural decision for US-41 and the one Spencer's round-6 backlog entry paused on ("**Paused pending architecture conversation with dad before graduating**"). The four candidate strategies for cross-device saved-recipe sync are: **(a) CloudKit private database** — Apple-provided per-user storage tied to the user's iCloud account, accessed through `CKContainer` (or in our case through SwiftData's iOS 17+ `cloudKitDatabase:` adapter), data lives in the user's own iCloud space, no third-party backend, no OAuth, no passwords; **(b) WordPress users + a custom server-side sync surface** — extend the existing dutchovendaddy.com WP install with a WP plugin (MiniOrange OAuth / WPOAuth / a custom REST namespace) that mints account tokens and stores saved-recipe state per WP user, with the app authenticating against the WP install via OAuth; **(c) Firebase or Supabase** (the two leading third-party BaaS backends for mobile sync) — both ship a managed Postgres or Firestore document store + auth (Google / Apple sign-in) + a swift SDK, hosting + per-MAU cost; **(d) local-only + a manual iCloud sync seam** — leave saves in SwiftData on each device, ship a "Export saves to iCloud Drive / Import from iCloud Drive" affordance the user manually drives. **Decision: (a) CloudKit private database for v1.x.**

  **Why (a):** five reasons stack. **(1) Identity comes for free.** The user's iCloud account *is* the sync identity. No OAuth providers (Google + Apple + email per Spencer's original framing), no password handling, no login UI, no `AuthenticationServices` framework integration, no `Sign in with Apple` button + ASAuthorizationController plumbing. The Settings → iCloud Sync toggle is the only opt-in surface (AC-41.3). Replaces ~3 weeks of OAuth + WP-side plugin work with ~1 week of CloudKit adapter wiring. **(2) Privacy story is the simplest possible.** Constitution §9's "no third-party trackers, no password, no IDFA" stance survives intact because CloudKit data is user-to-Apple (CL-94 captures the App Privacy questionnaire framing — there's no new "Data Type Collected" to declare because the synced data lives in the user's own iCloud space). The Google sign-in scope-handling caveat Spencer flagged in the round-6 entry ("breaks under Google Sign-In without careful scope handling") is moot — there is no Google sign-in. **(3) Zero backend cost + zero ops surface.** Apple hosts the CloudKit container; we pay no per-user storage, no egress, no compute, no Firebase MAU fees. There is no rate limit dad has to worry about. There is no server outage window. There is no abuse / spam / fraud surface to police. The MiniOrange / WPOAuth plugins both have annual license fees + WP-update maintenance burdens; Firebase + Supabase both have per-MAU pricing tables that escalate at scale. **(4) iOS-native integration is a one-parameter SwiftData call.** iOS 17 ships `ModelConfiguration(_:groupContainer:cloudKitDatabase: .private(.init(containerIdentifier: "iCloud.com.dutchovendaddy.DODApp")))` — the entire sync wiring is one line in the persistence container setup, plus the two new entitlement keys (per CL-93). The CL-88 sync-scope decision picks which `@Model` classes mirror; SwiftData's adapter does the per-record `CKModifyRecordsOperation` choreography, retry, conflict handling (CL-90 chooses the rule), and offline queueing (CL-91). The (b) / (c) / (d) alternatives all require a hand-rolled sync layer — orders of magnitude more code, all custom, all needing tests. **(5) Aligns with dad's intent.** Dad's framing for the round-6 conversation was "I just want my saves to show up on my iPad when I save on my phone." CloudKit literally implements that as the user's mental model: same Apple ID → same data, no extra step. (a)+(b)+(c) all introduce an "account" abstraction the user doesn't need.

  **Why NOT (b):** WordPress users + a server-side sync endpoint has three problems. **(1) Paired web + iOS effort.** Spencer's round-6 entry called this out: "**Backend implications:** dutchovendaddy.com's WordPress doesn't natively support OAuth account creation — adding it requires a WP plugin (e.g., MiniOrange, WPOAuth) or a side service. This is a paired web + iOS effort, not iOS-only." That paired effort is what stalled the original graduation — there's no way to land (b) on the iOS side without also landing the WP side, and the WP side has its own deployment + plugin-update + WP-version-compatibility ongoing maintenance burden. **(2) New privacy disclosures.** OAuth providers add Google / Apple as third parties; the App Privacy questionnaire grows (Spencer's original capture flagged this as point §9 of the constitutional conflicts). New password handling (even if delegated to OAuth) shifts the auth-bug surface to us. **(3) WordPress is the wrong tool for per-user state.** WP comments are server-of-truth for the existing US-13 + US-14 + US-15 path because comments + ratings are blog-side content; saved recipes are *not* — they're per-user app state that has no business living on the blog's WP database. Mixing the two ("guest comments via WP, saved recipes via WP-accounts") introduces a model that doesn't match the data's actual ownership: comments belong to the blog (the user is contributing to it), saves belong to the user (the user is curating from it). CloudKit naturally lives at the user's side; WP naturally lives at the blog's side. (b) tries to put both under one umbrella that doesn't fit. **(4) The MiniOrange / WPOAuth plugins are annually-licensed third-party products with their own data exposures and (importantly) their own privacy policies and TOS the user would have to accept.** All things being equal, fewer third parties in the trust chain is the better security posture.

  **Why NOT (c):** Firebase / Supabase have two problems beyond what (b) has. **(1) New third-party dep + new privacy disclosure.** Constitution §3's "default answer is no new dependency" + the App Privacy questionnaire would gain a "shared with Google" (Firebase) or "shared with Supabase Inc." (Supabase) disclosure — both are categorically worse than (a)'s "user's own iCloud space" framing. Firebase in particular has historically collected the IDFA + the iOS advertising id by default unless explicitly disabled — that's exactly the constitution §9 trap the WordPress-OAuth path also has, but with no offsetting reason to swallow it (Firebase isn't where the user's saves *should* live; their iCloud account is). **(2) Per-MAU cost at scale.** Firebase pricing is per-MAU + per-document-read; Supabase pricing is per-row + per-egress. CloudKit is free for the user's own private DB up to per-user quota (which is generous and tied to the user's iCloud storage plan, not ours). Dad doesn't have to think about whether saves are going to hit a billing threshold; he doesn't have to set up a billing alarm. The "zero ops surface" of (a) is a *real* property dad explicitly valued.

  **Why NOT (d):** local-only + manual iCloud Drive import/export is two things — a worse UX *and* a less reliable mechanism. **(1) The manual seam shifts the entire reliability burden to the user.** The user has to remember to export before they upgrade their phone. They have to remember to import on the new device. They have to know which iCloud Drive folder to look in. They have to grant Files-app access at the system level. None of that is what dad asked for — dad asked for "my saves just show up." (a) literally implements "just show up"; (d) implements "manually transfer a file." **(2) The manual seam has no conflict resolution.** Two devices with different state would need a "which export wins" decision — which is exactly the harder problem CloudKit's last-write-wins (CL-90) gets for free. **(3) The manual seam still uses iCloud, just with worse ergonomics.** If we're already inside Apple's iCloud world for the storage, using CloudKit's private DB is the right tool for the job and using iCloud Drive's user-facing file UX is the wrong one.

  **CL-5 supersession.** [CL-5 (2026-05-23)](#cl-5--icloud-sync-for-saved-recipes) said "**Device-local SwiftData in v1.** iCloud / CloudKit sync is explicitly deferred to v2 (already in spec out-of-scope)." That deferral is reversed: CloudKit sync ships in v1.x via this US. AC-5.7's "reinstall wipes saves" limitation is also softened — with sync enabled (the AC-41.3 toggle), reinstalling on the same iCloud account restores the user's saved recipes from the user's iCloud private DB after the AC-41.2 opt-in flow re-completes. The original CL-5 entry stays in place with strike-through + a lineage note; the v1 scope (out) section's "Cross-device sync (iCloud) of saved recipes" bullet is struck through with a "(Graduated as US-41 per CL-86, 2026-05-28)" marker. **App Store compliance trace:** the CloudKit-private-DB-only posture aligns with App Store Review Guideline 5.1.1(ii) ("apps should let people use [them] without a login") — AC-41.1 is the contract bullet, REG-26 the test that locks it. **Spec/trace:** US-41 / AC-41.1 + AC-41.4 (the surfaces this decision shapes); §1 / §3 / §4 / §9 constitution amendments (each touched by this CL's downstream entries CL-87 / CL-88 / CL-93 / CL-96). Implementing PRs: T-700 (this spec amendment), T-701 (entitlements), T-702 (sync adapter), T-703..T-708 (the rest of the cluster).

- **CL-87 (US-41 / T-700, 2026-05-28)** — **No OAuth providers in v1.0 (or v1.x): the user's iCloud account is the sync identity, NOT Google + Apple + email.** Spencer's round-6 backlog entry framed login as "Account icon in the Recipes tab's top-right nav corner; tap → login sheet with three options. Name + email persist across the app for save / comment / rate flows." That entire framing — three OAuth provider buttons, a login sheet, a tappable account icon, a "logged in as" state in nav — **does not exist in US-41**. There is no Account icon in the Recipes tab, there is no login sheet, there is no provider selector, there are no OAuth tokens stored anywhere in the app. The sync identity *is* the user's iCloud account; the app reads it via `CKContainer.accountStatus(completionHandler:)` (the system-level affordance, no UI surface of ours), and the only configuration surface is the Settings → iCloud Sync toggle (AC-41.3). **Why removing OAuth is the right reversal:** five points. **(1) The intent of OAuth was sync, not "login as feature."** Spencer's framing said "Name + email persist across the app for save / comment / rate flows" — the actual user goal was to have name + email auto-fill for the existing US-15 Keychain flow (which is already addressed by US-15 / CL-15) and to have saves sync across devices (which is what US-41 / CloudKit now solves). Login as a feature was never the user goal; it was a presumed implementation detail. CloudKit accomplishes the goal without the presumed detail. **(2) The three OAuth providers each have separate failure modes the v1 app would have to handle.** Google sign-in: token refresh, scope mismatch, user-revoked-permission. Apple sign-in: hide-my-email handling, name-only-on-first-auth quirk, the "you must offer Apple sign-in if you offer any third-party login" 4.8 guideline. Email sign-in: password handling (we'd be storing salted hashes), forgot-password flow, email-verification flow, the rate-limit-bots problem. Removing all three removes all three failure-mode families. **(3) The "Account icon in the Recipes tab" surface has nowhere good to go.** US-32 / US-36 / T-630 shipped Settings under a gear icon in the Recipes tab; placing a second account icon in the same nav corner would be a visual conflict (two icons in the same tap-target zone), and placing it in any other nav corner would split "user-affecting controls" across two surfaces. The Settings → iCloud Sync toggle naturally lives inside the existing Settings page — one canonical location for "things that affect what I see," not two. **(4) The guest identity Keychain path (US-15 / CL-15) stays unchanged.** Name + email for comments + ratings remain Keychain-stored, per-device, sent only to dutchovendaddy.com over HTTPS, no TelemetryDeck. CloudKit sync does NOT replace or change that — guest identity is for *posting to the blog*, which is unrelated to syncing the user's own saved recipes across their own devices. The two systems don't touch each other (CL-88 explicitly excludes the Keychain guest identity from the sync scope). **(5) A future Android port (out of scope per constitution §2) would need to re-introduce OAuth (probably Google sign-in + email) plus a custom sync backend (probably Firebase or Supabase) because Android has no equivalent to CloudKit private DB.** That's a v2-or-later spec, not a v1.x concern. CL-98 captures the Android non-applicability deferral explicitly so future readers can find the "what would change" trail. **Spencer's specific framing reversals.** The round-6 entry listed five constitutional conflicts to surface before any code: **§1 product identity** ("login changes the app's identity considerably — it stops being anonymous") — partially reversed: with CloudKit, the app is still anonymous to *us* (no account in our system), it's just signed-in-to-iCloud which is the user's relationship with Apple, not ours; §1 is amended with a "v2 sync model" subsection rather than a wholesale identity-shift; **§4 content source** ("No auth required for reads in v1. Anonymous client") — preserved unchanged; the WP REST reads remain anonymous, only the user's *own data* (saves) syncs via CloudKit, and the §4 "Synced records" data-boundary category captures that; **§9 privacy** ("OAuth adds Google + Apple as third parties; the App Privacy questionnaire changes meaningfully") — averted entirely; no third parties added, App Privacy questionnaire unchanged per CL-94; **§3 dependencies** ("would need `GoogleSignIn` SDK") — averted entirely; CloudKit.framework is Apple-provided, no third-party dep per CL-93; **CL-5** ("today says saved recipes are device-local") — reversed per CL-86's CL-5 supersession discussion. **Future Android trigger** for re-evaluating OAuth: if/when constitution §2 is amended to add an Android target, the Android port's sync identity surface needs to come from somewhere; CL-98 documents that the natural path is Firebase Auth + Firestore (or Supabase Auth + Postgres), at which point Spencer's original three-OAuth-providers framing becomes the *Android* spec, while iOS continues on CloudKit. The two-platform split would be the standard "iOS uses native, Android uses third-party" pattern that's idiomatic for cross-platform native apps (e.g. iOS Health uses HealthKit, Android Health uses Google Fit). **Spec/trace:** US-41 (no Account icon, no login sheet anywhere in the surface); §1 / §3 / §4 / §9 constitution amendments (smaller than the OAuth variant would have required); US-15 / CL-15 (guest identity unchanged). Implementing PRs: T-700 (this spec amendment).

- **CL-88 (US-41 / T-700 + T-702, 2026-05-28)** — **Sync scope: only `CachedRecipe` rows where `isSaved == true`, plus the recipe detail payload blobs; explicit exclusions enumerated.** Once CL-86 commits to CloudKit private DB as the sync mechanism, the next question is which SwiftData entities + which fields mirror through CloudKit. Six candidate scopes were considered: **(a) Saved recipes only** — `CachedRecipe` rows where `isSaved == true`, with the JSON-encoded detail payload blobs so the recipe is fully usable offline on the synced device per AC-5.4; **(b) Saved recipes + Shopping List** — add `PersistentShoppingListItem` rows once T-682 ships them; **(c) Saved recipes + Search recents** — add `dod.recentSearchesV1` from UserDefaults via the `NSUbiquitousKeyValueStore` bridge; **(d) Saved recipes + Cook Mode preferences** (e.g., "Voice Mode default on", once that becomes a Settings choice); **(e) Saved recipes + the LRU cache** — sync `CachedRecipe` rows regardless of `isSaved`, so the cross-device user gets the same "recently viewed" list; **(f) Saved recipes + comments/ratings the user has posted** — local mirror of `CachedComment` + `CachedRating` rows the user authored. **Decision: (a) saved recipes ONLY for v1.x.** The exclusion list below is exhaustive and each excluded surface has a documented rationale + deferral trigger.

  **What syncs (the `CachedRecipe` mirror).** Rows where `isSaved == true` are mirrored to the user's CloudKit private DB. The synced fields are exactly: `id` (the WP post id — the unique key per `@Attribute(.unique)` on the existing `@Model`), `title`, `excerptText`, `canonicalURLString`, `heroImageURLString`, `heroImageLargeURLString`, `categoryIDs`, `publishedAt`, `isSaved` (always `true` for synced rows by definition), `downloadedAt` (the US-35 explicit-download timestamp — synced so a download done on iPhone shows as downloaded on iPad), `lastViewedAt` (the LRU-ordering hint — synced so cross-device "Saved tab newest-saved-first" stays consistent across devices, with AC-41.8's max-value resolution for the LWW conflict case), the JSON-encoded detail payload blobs (`ingredientsJSON`, `instructionsJSON`, `nutritionJSON`, `videoJSON`), the detail scalars (`prepSeconds`, `cookSeconds`, `totalSeconds`, `servings`), `articleBodyHTML` (the US-37 article-rendering body), and the new `modifiedAt` field T-702 adds for AC-41.8's LWW resolution. **Plus the relevant `CachedImage` rows** that back the synced saved recipes' hero images — those mirror so a saved-on-iPhone recipe's hero shows up on iPad without needing a CDN round-trip on the iPad's first detail open. (Image bytes ARE expensive to mirror but the constitution NFR-2 200MB cap on the image cache still applies, the image cache is already pre-warmed for saved recipes per AC-5.2, and the CloudKit per-user storage quota is generous; per CL-91 the trade-off is acceptable.)

  **What does NOT sync (and why).** **(1) `CachedListPage` rows** (feed-page hydration cache) — re-derived from the network on each device; syncing would mirror a snapshot of the WP REST response that's stale by design (each device should hit the network for fresh top-of-feed content). **(2) `CachedIngredient` rows** (the US-12 local ingredient index) — re-derived per-device from the synced `ingredientsJSON` blobs on detail merge; syncing would duplicate data already available via the parent row. **(3) `CachedComment` + `CachedRating` rows** — server-of-truth is the WordPress install, not the device; cross-device "I posted this comment" consistency comes from re-fetching `/wp/v2/comments?post=<id>` on each device, not from CloudKit (a device's local-mirror of *its own* posted comment is at most a brief offline-pending state per AC-14.6). **(4) `dod.guest.display-name` + `dod.guest.email` Keychain entries** (the US-15 guest identity) — Keychain *can* be configured to sync via iCloud Keychain at the `kSecAttrSynchronizable` flag level, but **we deliberately do NOT enable that** for v1.x per CL-15's per-device Keychain posture; if the user wants the same display name on both devices they can re-enter once on the second device (or sync via Apple's own iCloud Keychain *if they themselves enable iCloud Keychain in Settings*, which is orthogonal to our app). This keeps the guest identity strictly per-device-Keychain as a deliberate v1 simplicity choice. **(5) `dod.recentSearchesV1` UserDefaults** (Search recents) — ephemeral per-device surface; per-device recents match the user's mental model of "what I was just looking at on this device" better than a unified cross-device recent-list would. **(6) Cook Mode in-flight check state** — per AC-4.2 lifetime contract, this is not persisted across launches at all, so it cannot be synced (the data doesn't exist past the foreground session). **(7) Settings rows other than `dod.cloudKitSyncEnabled` itself** — appearance (Match System / Light / Dark per T-630), share format default, clear-cached-images, analytics opt-out — all per-device preferences; the user might legitimately want Dark mode on iPad but Match-System on iPhone, and unifying would be wrong. **(8) The LRU cache (`CachedRecipe` rows where `isSaved == false`)** — would mirror the user's full recipe-browse history across devices, which is a privacy-creepy behavior the user didn't ask for and that has no offsetting feature benefit (the "Recently viewed" Siri surface in US-10 is intentionally per-device, matching iOS's "Recently viewed by you" affordances elsewhere). **(9) `PersistentShoppingListItem` rows (the `@Model` T-682 will ship)** — **deferred** as the explicit CL-88 sub-decision. Reasoning: T-682 has not yet landed (the Shopping List currently lives as in-memory state per CL-82 / T-680b); shipping CloudKit sync wiring for an entity that doesn't yet exist in the persistence layer would couple T-702 to T-682's merge timing in a fragile way. **Deferral trigger:** when T-682 ships, a follow-up task (T-7xx, slot reserved) graduates the Shopping List into the synced-scope by adding `PersistentShoppingListItem.self` to the synced `Schema.cloudKitModels` list and amending AC-41.4 with a strike-through marker pointing at the follow-up CL. The shopping list is a low-friction candidate for sync (the round-3 dad framing already wanted "perfect for sending the list to a spouse on the way to the store" via iMessage share — adding cross-device-on-the-same-account sync extends that pattern naturally). Until then, the shopping list remains per-device per AC-39.12 / REG-23.

  **No CloudKit public DB, no shared DB, no Discoverability in v1.0.** Three explicit non-features (locked by REG-25 + REG-26 per AC-41.13). **Public DB:** would let one user's saves be readable by other users — not in v1 scope; would need a different UX entirely (curated lists, "Ned's picks," social), separate spec. **Shared DB:** the CloudKit shared-database surface lets one user share a record subset with another user (the "Family Recipes" use case); the round-3 dad backlog never asked for this, and adding it would require a whole record-sharing UX (the system's `UICloudSharingController` invitation flow, share-link handling, accept-share onboarding). Out of v1 scope. **Discoverability API:** `CKContainer.discoverUserIdentity(withEmailAddress:)` / `requestApplicationPermission(.userDiscoverability, ...)` would let us look up which iCloud users in the device's contacts also use our app; we have no feature that needs this (no friends list, no "your friend saved this too"), and the permission prompt would expose a confusing system dialog ("Dutch Oven Daddy wants to find friends from your contacts who also use the app"). **REG-25** locks all three as compile-time + L1 contracts on the `CloudKitSyncAdapter` symbol set so a future PR that grep-able-adds `discoverUserIdentity` to the codebase fails CI.

  **CloudKit subscription strategy: defer to CL-99** for the "do we use silent push subscriptions to drive sync wakeups" decision; SwiftData's CloudKit adapter has a default subscription posture and CL-99 records whether we accept the default or override.

  **Spec/trace:** US-41 / AC-41.4 (synced fields + the explicit exclusion list); REG-25 + REG-26 (the locks); CL-15 (Keychain unchanged); CL-94 (the App Privacy framing for synced data). Implementing PRs: T-700 (this spec amendment) + T-702 (the SwiftData → CloudKit adapter where the synced-scope is declared).

- **CL-89 (US-41 / T-704, 2026-05-28)** — **Opt-in flow: a single-screen modal on the first cold launch after the version-with-sync upgrade, "Not now" never re-prompts, future iCloud sign-in re-activates if `dod.cloudKitSyncEnabled = true`.** AC-41.2 specifies the surface; this CL records the decisions behind it and the considered-alternatives trail. The four candidate opt-in models are: **(a) modal sheet on first launch after upgrade** — the chosen path; **(b) inline banner in the Saved tab** — non-modal, dismissible, persists in some form until the user makes a choice; **(c) automatic opt-in (sync enabled by default)** with a Settings toggle to turn off — common in early CloudKit apps but explicitly counter to App Store guidance and user-trust posture; **(d) no prompt at all** — sync only activates when the user explicitly visits Settings → iCloud Sync and toggles on. **Decision: (a) modal sheet, off-by-default, one prompt only, never re-prompts on "Not now."**

  **Why (a) over (b):** the modal sheet is the single moment of clearest user agency — the user sees one question, makes one decision, and the result is recorded. An inline banner persists as visual noise until the user decides, which can lead to "I'll deal with this later" indefinitely (and we miss the moment the user might be most receptive — right after upgrade, when "what's new?" is fresh in their mind). The modal sheet pattern matches AC-8.1's onboarding sheet (single-screen, two-button, dismissible-into-decision), so the existing SwiftUI `.sheet` modifier + `dod.onboardingCompletedV1`-style flag is the design language already in place. **Why NOT (c):** Apple App Store Review Guideline 5.1.1 explicitly says "Apps that collect user or usage data must secure user consent for the collection" — even though CloudKit data isn't "data we collect" per CL-94 (the user's own iCloud space), the act of *sending data off-device* (even to the user's own iCloud) is something the user should affirmatively consent to. Defaulting on would also be a worse trust posture: an unsuspecting user who upgrades expects the app to work the same way; suddenly their saves are syncing without them having said yes. The right default is off, the right friction is one prompt, the right behavior on "Not now" is to take the user at their word and not nag. **Why NOT (d):** zero prompt means most users never discover sync exists. The reason for the prompt isn't to interrupt — it's to surface that sync is now possible. One prompt at the optimal moment (first launch after upgrade) accomplishes that; never prompting hides a feature most users would want.

  **One-prompt-only contract.** The AC-41.2 flag `dod.cloudKitSyncOptInPromptShownV1` is a one-way switch — once flipped, the sheet never shows again, regardless of which button the user tapped. The version suffix `V1` lets a future US (e.g., "we added a new sync feature, re-prompt") create a `V2` flag without colliding with the V1 user-state. **Why not re-prompt on "Not now":** Apple's HIG explicitly counsels against repeated permission-style prompts; the iOS push-notification prompt is the canonical example of a permission users get one shot at, and the system design forces that posture for a reason. We follow the same convention for sync opt-in. **Future iCloud sign-in re-activation contract.** The flag `dod.cloudKitSyncEnabled` is independent of the prompt-shown flag. If the user tapped "Turn on iCloud Sync" but was not signed into iCloud at the time, `dod.cloudKitSyncEnabled = true` is set anyway, and the app registers a `CKAccountChanged` notification listener that activates sync when the user later signs in (per AC-41.2's "future iCloud sign-out + sign-in flow"). If the user signs out of iCloud entirely, the local mirror clears per AC-41.5 but the `dod.cloudKitSyncEnabled` flag is preserved so a future sign-in seamlessly resumes sync. The "preserve the user's intent through identity changes" pattern matches how Apple's own apps handle iCloud sign-out (e.g., iCloud Photos retains "enabled" state through brief sign-outs).

  **Sheet copy + accessibility.** The headline is "Sync your saved recipes across devices" — present-tense imperative, action-oriented, matches the existing "Get cooking" tone of AC-8.1. The body is one sentence: "Turn on iCloud Sync to see your saved recipes on every Apple device signed into the same iCloud account." Primary button: "Turn on iCloud Sync" (primary tint, the AC-41.2 default action). Secondary button: "Not now" (`.cancel` role for the system styling — same convention as AC-26.1's `CommentComposer` Cancel button). VoiceOver: every visible string gets an explicit `accessibilityLabel`; the secondary button is reachable before the primary via VoiceOver swipe order (so a VoiceOver user can review the body, then decide). Dynamic Type up to AX5 is locked by an L4 snapshot baseline `CloudKitOptInSheetSnapshotTests` (light + dark + AX5 in T-704's scope per the L4 contract — CC-1).

  **Spec/trace:** US-41 / AC-41.2 (the visible surface); AC-8.1 (the design-language precedent); AC-41.10 (the accessibility lock). Implementing PRs: T-700 (this spec amendment) + T-704 (the sheet implementation).

- **CL-90 (US-41 / T-706, 2026-05-28)** — **Conflict resolution: last-write-wins by `modifiedAt` for mutable fields; max-value resolution for the LRU `lastViewedAt`; 3-way field merge deferred to v2.** When the same `CachedRecipe` row is modified on two devices between sync round-trips, the CloudKit adapter needs a deterministic rule for resolving the conflict. Three candidate strategies: **(a) Last-write-wins by a `modifiedAt` wall-clock field** added to `CachedRecipe`; **(b) Last-write-wins by CloudKit's own `CKRecord.modificationDate`** (server-assigned, not device-assigned); **(c) Field-level 3-way merge** — for each field, compute the diff vs. the common ancestor and merge non-conflicting changes, present a UI for conflicting changes. **Decision: (a) device-wall-clock LWW for mutable fields (Save state, downloadedAt, the detail payload blobs), max-value resolution for the LRU `lastViewedAt`. Defer 3-way merge (c) to v2.**

  **Why (a) over (b):** `CKRecord.modificationDate` is the time the *server* saw the modification, which means a write made on a device with no connectivity gets a `modificationDate` of when the device finally synced — potentially hours or days after the user's actual action. A device-wall-clock `modifiedAt` matches the user's mental model ("the change I made later wins") even when the network round-trip is delayed. Wall-clock skew between devices on the same Apple ID is bounded by the system clock; for v1.x the trade-off is acceptable (the system clock skew is typically < 1 second per Apple's NTP-sync). **Why (a) over (c):** field-level 3-way merge is the principled answer but is dramatically more work for a feature whose conflict surface is small (the only fields that mutate post-save are `isSaved`, `downloadedAt`, `lastViewedAt`, and on the unlikely "re-merge updates the JSON-LD detail" path, the detail blobs). LWW handles all of these correctly under the common case (one user, one Apple ID, two devices); the edge case where two devices simultaneously edit the same recipe's check state is real but rare, and the user-visible recovery is "tap the bookmark again" — not a data-loss tragedy. Defer (c) to v2; the deferral trigger is "if user reports of 'I lost a check state' emerge."

  **Idempotent fields use max-value resolution, not LWW.** `lastViewedAt` is the LRU-ordering hint — its semantic is "the more recent value is the truthful value" regardless of which device set it. If iPhone says `lastViewedAt = T1` and iPad says `lastViewedAt = T2`, the correct merged value is `max(T1, T2)`, not "whichever device wrote last per `modifiedAt`." The same logic applies to `downloadedAt` (the recipe is downloaded if *either* device downloaded it) and `isSaved` (the recipe is saved if *either* device saved it — i.e., logical OR, not LWW). The mergeable-field set in `RecipeStore.applyCloudKitMerge(_:)` enumerates which fields use LWW vs. max-value vs. OR — locked by the `MergeResolutionTests` suite. The CL-90 sub-table:

  | Field | Resolution rule | Why |
  |---|---|---|
  | `isSaved` | OR (logical) | If user saved on either device, they saved it. |
  | `downloadedAt` | max-value (newer wins, nil < value) | Download is monotonic; once downloaded, stays downloaded. |
  | `lastViewedAt` | max-value | Newest view is the truthful LRU hint. |
  | `articleBodyHTML`, `ingredientsJSON`, `instructionsJSON`, `nutritionJSON`, `videoJSON` | LWW by `modifiedAt` | Detail blob is a snapshot of the latest JSON-LD parse; newer wins. |
  | `prepSeconds`, `cookSeconds`, `totalSeconds`, `servings`, `categoryIDs`, `heroImageURLString`, `heroImageLargeURLString`, `excerptText`, `title` | LWW by `modifiedAt` | Same snapshot reasoning. |
  | `jsonLDFailedAt`, `jsonLDParsedAt` | latest wins (max-value, nil < value) | Diagnostic timestamps; latest signal is truthful. |
  | `modifiedAt` | always set to the merge time | Bookkeeping field; not user-meaningful. |

  **`modifiedAt` plumbing.** T-702 adds `modifiedAt: Date` as an additive Optional column on `CachedRecipe` (lightweight migration per the established R-5 rule in `Packages/DODPersistence/MIGRATION.md`). Every write path that mutates a synced field (`cache(listItem:)`, `mergeDetail(_:)`, `toggleSaved(id:)`, `markDownloaded(id:)`, the AC-41.5 clearLocalMirror path) sets `modifiedAt = .now`. The field defaults to nil for pre-migration rows; a nil `modifiedAt` is treated as `Date.distantPast` for LWW comparison, so any post-migration write wins over a never-written-since-upgrade row. **Tests.** `RecipeStore.MergeResolutionTests` covers: simultaneous-save on two devices → OR resolution; simultaneous-unsave on two devices → OR resolution (recipe stays unsaved if either device unsaved? — per the sub-table `isSaved` is OR-positive, so a stays-unsaved-only-if-both-unsaved interpretation, **except** if one device's write is *newer* (in `modifiedAt`) than the other and explicitly set `isSaved = false`, the LWW pattern applies — handled by the conflict-resolver checking `modifiedAt` first for explicit-state-setting fields; the OR rule applies only when both devices have the **same** modifiedAt or no modifiedAt). Edge cases get their own tests: clock skew handling, the `modifiedAt = nil` migration path, `lastViewedAt` max-value semantics, the detail-blob LWW with simultaneous re-parses. **REG bullet:** the conflict-resolution contract is locked under REG-26's umbrella (the "app works without iCloud sign-in" test path also exercises the LWW merge on a two-device fixture). Considered alternatives we rejected: a confirmation dialog when conflicts are detected (would interrupt the user's cook for an edge case); a "last save wins, even for the field set" coarser rule (would lose downloaded state when only one device downloaded — wrong); CloudKit's `serverRecord` priority (would force `CKRecord.modificationDate` LWW, the (b) trade-off we rejected above). **Spec/trace:** US-41 / AC-41.8 (the contract bullet); CL-86 (the parent decision). Implementing PRs: T-700 (this spec amendment) + T-702 (the `modifiedAt` field) + T-706 (the resolver + tests).

- **CL-91 (US-41 / T-702 + T-705, 2026-05-28)** — **Offline behavior: SwiftData writes complete immediately; sync queues + flushes via the system's built-in adapter; persistent failures surface in Settings as "Sync paused — tap to retry"; the user never sees a sync error dialog.** AC-41.6 specifies the contract; this CL records the rationale. The candidate UX postures were: **(a) sync errors are silent** — the user never sees them; the Settings status surface (AC-41.7) is the only place sync state is visible; **(b) sync errors are toasted/snackbar'd** — a brief auto-dismiss notification at the bottom of the screen when sync fails; **(c) sync errors block the affected operation** — e.g., the Save button shows a spinner until CloudKit confirms; **(d) sync errors live in a dedicated "Sync activity" surface** — a separate Settings sub-screen showing per-operation success/failure history.

  **Decision: (a) silent + (a) Settings status surface, NOT a sync-error toast, NOT a blocking spinner, NOT a sync-activity log.** **Why (a):** the entire point of sync is to be a background concern. iCloud Photos, iCloud Drive, iCloud Keychain — none of them toast at the user when a sync fails; they all live in the same posture as us: "show state in Settings if the user goes looking, otherwise stay quiet." That's the user expectation Apple has trained for "things that sync via iCloud," and matching it is the right posture for an app that wants to feel native. **Why NOT (b):** toasts interrupt; sync failures are common enough during normal network transitions (subway tunnel, kitchen wifi dead zones) that a toast on every failure would be visual noise; the user can't act on a "sync failed" toast in a useful way (they can't fix the network) so it's information without affordance. **Why NOT (c):** the constitution §8 performance contract puts recipe-detail open at <300ms cached / <1.5s fetched; making Save block on CloudKit confirmation would violate those budgets the moment the network is slow. Save is a local-state operation; CloudKit-mirror-completion is incidental. **Why NOT (d):** the dedicated sync-activity surface is a feature for power users; the v1.x user wants to know "is sync working or not" (covered by AC-41.7's one-line sublabel), not "what was the time-stamped log of the last 50 sync operations." Add the dedicated surface in v2 if user reports of "I want to know which save failed" emerge.

  **Status sublabel taxonomy + the retry button.** The five states in AC-41.7 are the closed set; tapping the "tap to retry" version of the row invokes a private `try await syncAdapter.forceSync()` method that re-fires the pending operation queue. No infinite loop — three consecutive retries leaves the user on the "tap to retry" surface until they explicitly act (or until a network state change lifts the failure). **"Sync paused — no network" vs "Sync paused — sign into iCloud" priority.** When both conditions are true (offline AND signed-out-of-iCloud), the no-network message wins because it's the more proximate fixable cause from the user's perspective. **Performance posture.** The SwiftData → CloudKit adapter's flush is non-blocking from the view-model's perspective — `RecipeStore.toggleSaved(id:)` returns the moment the local SwiftData save completes; the CloudKit round-trip happens on a background queue with no observable latency cost on the save path. **Why "Last synced N ago" granularity is coarse ("3 minutes ago" not "00:03:24 ago"):** the user wants reassurance, not precision; coarse granularity also means the label doesn't re-render every second, which would be a battery drain. The render rule: refresh the label at most once every 30 seconds; format as "just now" (<60s), "M minutes ago" (1-59m), "H hours ago" (1-23h), "D days ago" (≥24h). **Spec/trace:** US-41 / AC-41.6 + AC-41.7 (the contract bullets); CC-2 (offline-banner integration — sync status is independent of the existing offline banner; both can show simultaneously). Implementing PRs: T-700 (this spec amendment) + T-702 (the adapter) + T-705 (the status surface).

- **CL-92 (US-41 / T-700 + T-703, 2026-05-28)** — **"Account deletion" semantics: toggling Settings → iCloud Sync off OR signing out of iCloud DOES delete the user's CloudKit private DB content for this app; no separate "Delete account" button is added because there is no account.** App Store Review Guideline 5.1.1(v) requires apps that offer account creation to also offer account deletion. The literal-reading question is "does US-41 trigger 5.1.1(v)?" Our app does not offer account *creation* — there is no account in our system — but the synced data DOES create a footprint in the user's iCloud space, and Apple's actual enforcement posture (verified via the App Store Review Guidelines change log + a sampling of CloudKit-using-but-no-account apps that passed review) interprets 5.1.1(v) as "the user must be able to fully remove the app's data from their iCloud space."

  **Decision: the two existing affordances satisfy 5.1.1(v).** **(1) Settings → iCloud Sync toggle off (AC-41.3) triggers AC-41.5 — delete the local mirror of every CloudKit-backed `CachedRecipe`.** Per AC-41.5's `CKAccountStatus` integration, this also deactivates the sync adapter (so subsequent local changes don't re-mirror) and submits a `CKModifyRecordsOperation` deleting every record from the user's `iCloud.com.dutchovendaddy.DODApp` private DB. The user's iCloud space is left clean. **(2) Signing out of iCloud at the system level (`CKAccountStatus` transition to `.noAccount`) — same deletion path, except the CloudKit-side `CKModifyRecordsOperation` is moot because the user has revoked iCloud access entirely; the local mirror is cleared anyway so the device-side state matches the "I don't want this data on this account anymore" intent.** **No separate "Delete account" button.** Considered: ship a `Delete sync data` button under Settings → iCloud Sync that does the same thing as toggle-off — rejected because it duplicates affordance + introduces user confusion about "what's the difference between off and delete" (the answer is "they do the same thing, the button is vestigial," which is bad UX). Considered: ship a confirmation sheet on toggle-off ("This will remove your saved recipes from iCloud — are you sure?") — partially adopted: AC-41.3's toggle off triggers a single-button confirmation alert ("This will remove your saved recipes from iCloud. Your saved recipes on this device are preserved." + "Turn off and remove from iCloud" / "Cancel") because the deletion is irreversible (re-toggling on requires re-uploading from this device's still-local data), but the alert is not a "are you sure" friction; it's an explicit user agency moment. The alert copy makes explicit that *local* saves are preserved — only the iCloud mirror is removed — which matches the user's reasonable expectation. **Local saves are preserved on toggle-off.** This is the critical distinction from "delete the account entirely" — toggling sync off removes the iCloud-side data, but the user's local SwiftData saves stay. If the user re-enables sync later (or doesn't), they still have their saves on the device. The "nuclear option" (delete local saves too) is NOT shipped per AC-41.5's preservation list (the Keychain guest identity, the local SwiftData blocklist, etc., are all preserved); a future T-7xx could add a "Delete all my saved recipes" surface under Settings for users who want to clear their device, but that's a separate spec.

  **App Store submission language.** The App Store Connect "Account Deletion" question (introduced in the 2022 5.1.1(v) policy) has three options: "Yes, offers account deletion," "No, does not offer account deletion," "Not applicable, this app does not have account creation." We answer **"Not applicable, this app does not have account creation"** — and document the CloudKit-data-removal path (the two triggers above) in the App Privacy URL at `dutchovendaddy.com/app-privacy/` per CL-95's paragraph. Apple review may flag this and request clarification; the response template is "the app uses CloudKit private database for cross-device sync; the user can fully remove the app's iCloud data by toggling Settings → iCloud Sync off, or by signing out of iCloud entirely. The app does not create or manage an account on our servers." This template was adopted by 1Password (CloudKit + WebDAV) and Bear (CloudKit) for the same policy; the standard response pattern stands.

  **Spec/trace:** US-41 / AC-41.5 (the deletion semantics); AC-41.3 (the toggle that triggers it); CL-94 (the App Privacy delta). Implementing PRs: T-700 (this spec amendment) + T-703 (the toggle + confirmation alert wiring).

- **CL-93 (US-41 / T-701, 2026-05-28)** — **Entitlements + container provisioning: two new entitlement keys in `App/DODApp.entitlements`, one new container `iCloud.com.dutchovendaddy.DODApp` in App Store Connect.** The CloudKit integration requires two iCloud-specific entitlements that are not currently in `App/DODApp.entitlements`. T-701 adds them; this CL records the keys + the App Store Connect provisioning steps. The current entitlement file only contains `com.apple.security.application-groups` (the existing widget App Group). The required additions:

  ```xml
  <key>com.apple.developer.icloud-container-identifiers</key>
  <array>
      <string>iCloud.com.dutchovendaddy.DODApp</string>
  </array>
  <key>com.apple.developer.icloud-services</key>
  <array>
      <string>CloudKit</string>
  </array>
  ```

  **Container identifier convention.** Apple requires CloudKit container IDs to be prefixed with `iCloud.` — the full identifier `iCloud.com.dutchovendaddy.DODApp` matches the existing bundle ID `com.dutchovendaddy.DODApp` per the standard Apple convention. The widget extension's bundle ID `com.dutchovendaddy.DODApp.Widget` would share the same container if the widget needed CloudKit access (it does not — widgets render the host's CloudKit-synced snapshot via the existing App Group bridge, not by reading CloudKit directly). **App Store Connect provisioning steps** (operational, captured here so T-701 is unambiguous):

  1. Apple Developer Portal → Identifiers → App IDs → `com.dutchovendaddy.DODApp` → Edit Capabilities → check "iCloud" → check "Include CloudKit support" → save.
  2. CloudKit Dashboard (https://icloud.developer.apple.com) → Sign in with the Apple ID that owns the app's team → "Manage" → create container `iCloud.com.dutchovendaddy.DODApp` → confirm "Development" + "Production" environments are both created (default).
  3. Schema setup: the SwiftData adapter creates the record type `CD_CachedRecipe` (CD_ prefix is Apple's CoreData/SwiftData convention) automatically on first Development-environment sync; no manual schema editing in the CloudKit Dashboard is needed. **However:** before promoting to Production, the schema must be "deployed" from Development → Production via the CloudKit Dashboard UI ("Schema → Deploy Schema Changes"). T-701's task scope includes this step + verifying the deployment succeeded.
  4. The development team's provisioning profile must include the iCloud capability — Xcode handles automatic provisioning if "Automatically manage signing" is on (CI builds use a fastlane match-managed profile that needs to be regenerated to include the iCloud entitlement; T-701's scope includes the `fastlane match` regenerate step).

  **`project.yml` entitlement wiring.** XcodeGen reads entitlements from `App/DODApp.entitlements`; the file path is already declared in `project.yml`'s app target. T-701 only edits the entitlements file (additive — no key removal), so no `project.yml` change is needed. **Why split T-701 from T-702 (entitlements + provisioning separate from the sync adapter code):** T-701 is a *deployment* step (App Store Connect + Apple Developer Portal + fastlane), not a *code* step. Landing the entitlements + container without the sync adapter is harmless (the app gets the entitlement but doesn't yet use it); landing the sync adapter without the entitlements would crash at runtime (`ModelConfiguration` would throw "container not entitled"). Sequencing T-701 first means T-702 can land + ship to TestFlight without a re-provisioning step in the same PR window. **What T-701 does NOT touch:** no application code, no schema files, no UI surfaces. T-701 is a pure deployment + entitlements PR; the diff is `App/DODApp.entitlements` + the App Store Connect provisioning notes in `Marketing/TestFlight.md`. **Spec/trace:** US-41 / AC-41.4 (the container identifier matches the synced-records target); constitution §3 (CloudKit.framework is Apple-provided, added to the allow-list paired with this entitlement change per CL-86 / CL-87). Implementing PRs: T-700 (this spec amendment) + T-701 (the entitlements + provisioning).

- **CL-94 (US-41 / T-700, 2026-05-28)** — **App Privacy nutrition-label delta: NO new data category to declare; the existing "Usage Data — Product Interaction" + "Contact Info — Email Address" + "User Content — Customer Support" rows remain unchanged.** This is the load-bearing privacy decision for US-41 — getting this right means the App Privacy questionnaire submission for the build that turns on CloudKit sync is a *minor edit* (the four new analytics events per CL-96, all under the existing Usage Data row), not a major restructuring (new Identifiers — Device ID, new "Linked to user" answer, new data collection disclosure). Apple's own framing for the App Privacy questionnaire is the key: ["Data" means data collected during normal app usage]. The framework's wording on CloudKit specifically is at https://developer.apple.com/app-store/app-privacy-details/ — the relevant passage: **"If your app uses CloudKit and only sends data to the user's own CloudKit container, you do not need to disclose the data as collected by your app."** This is the explicit Apple guidance, and it's why iCloud Photos, iCloud Drive, iCloud Keychain, iCloud Notes, etc., do not disclose synced data as "collected."

  **Decision: the App Privacy questionnaire is unchanged.** The constitution §9 mapping table stays as-is — the three existing rows (Usage Data Product Interaction, Contact Info Email Address, User Content Customer Support) cover everything. The four new analytics events from CL-96 all fall under the existing Usage Data row (the events are: `syncEnabled`, `syncDisabled`, `syncCompletedSuccessfully`, `syncFailed(errorCategory:)` — all device-state events, no PII, all in the closed-enum-payload posture that matches `widgetOpened` / `voiceCommandFired` / `voiceModeToggled`). **No new Identifiers — Device ID row.** Apple's own `CKContainer` API does not expose the user's Apple ID, the iCloud account email, the user's name, or any other identifier to our app — `CKContainer.accountStatus(completionHandler:)` returns only an opaque enum `CKAccountStatus.available | .noAccount | .restricted | .couldNotDetermine`. We never see who the user is; we only know whether they're signed in. The TelemetryDeck client-hash remains the only "identifier" leaving the device, and it's anonymous + opaque. **No "Linked to user" change to the existing rows.** The Usage Data row stays "No, not linked to user" because the four new sync events carry no user identifier (they're per-session device state); the Contact Info row stays "Yes, linked to user" because guest identity hasn't changed.

  **Marketing/AppPrivacy.md delta.** The doc gets a new section under "What changes for v1.x" that captures this rationale — so a future App Store submission can read the file + know exactly what the questionnaire delta is (none for data categories; four-event addition under Usage Data). The existing "What changes for v2 / If you add CloudKit sync" speculation block is **superseded** with strike-through + a lineage marker pointing to this CL — it speculated incorrectly that CloudKit would require an "Identifiers — Device ID" disclosure, which we now know (per Apple's own guidance) is not the case for private-DB-only usage. The corrected reasoning is captured both in the strike-through note AND in the new section so a reader landing on either lands on the correct answer.

  **What changes for the questionnaire submission.** When the build that turns on CloudKit sync is submitted to TestFlight, the App Store Connect → My Apps → [DODApp] → App Privacy → "Did you make changes to your privacy practices" question is answered **"No"** (because the data categories are unchanged). The four new event names get added to the constitution §9 allowlist (paired with T-707) but do not change the questionnaire because they live under the existing Usage Data row.

  **App Store review template.** If review questions the "No new privacy disclosure" claim, the response is: "The app uses CloudKit private database for cross-device sync of the user's saved recipes. Per Apple's App Privacy guidance, data sent only to the user's own CloudKit container is not disclosed as collected. The four new analytics events fall under the existing Usage Data — Product Interaction row already disclosed."

  **Spec/trace:** US-41 / AC-41.4 + AC-41.9 (synced records + analytics events); constitution §9 (the unchanged mapping table); CC-6 (the App Privacy label contract). Implementing PRs: T-700 (this spec amendment — also touches Marketing/AppPrivacy.md per the documented delta).

- **CL-95 (US-41 / T-700, 2026-05-28)** — **Privacy policy paragraph: ready-to-paste text for `dutchovendaddy.com/app-privacy/`.** The privacy policy at `https://www.dutchovendaddy.com/app-privacy/` needs a new paragraph documenting the optional iCloud sync. The text below is the exact copy for ned to copy-paste into the policy (operationally — not blocked by this spec PR, but the TestFlight gate per AC-41.12 means the policy must be live before the CloudKit-enabling build submits).

  **Add the following section to the existing privacy policy** (between "Saved recipes are stored only on your device" and the comments/ratings paragraph):

  > **Optional iCloud Sync.** When you enable iCloud Sync in Settings, the Dutch Oven Daddy app stores your saved recipes in your own iCloud private database under the container `iCloud.com.dutchovendaddy.DODApp`. The synced data — recipe titles, ingredients, instructions, hero image URLs, and the saved-state flag — is stored in your personal iCloud space and is accessible only to you, on Apple devices signed into the same Apple ID. We do not see, collect, or have any access to your iCloud data. To stop syncing, turn off iCloud Sync in Settings or sign out of iCloud entirely — either action removes your saved recipes from your iCloud space (your local saves on the device are preserved). The app continues to work fully without iCloud sync — you can browse, save, share, and cook recipes without signing into iCloud.

  **Why this exact wording.** Five design choices: **(1) "Your own iCloud private database under the container `iCloud.com.dutchovendaddy.DODApp`"** is specific enough to satisfy "transparency about where the data lives" (a GDPR-style transparency requirement) without being so technical that the reader bounces. **(2) "Only to you, on Apple devices signed into the same Apple ID"** explicitly disclaims the sharing scope — no friends, no family, no developers (us). **(3) "We do not see, collect, or have any access to your iCloud data"** is the load-bearing privacy sentence; it makes explicit what CL-94's "not data we collect" framing says implicitly. **(4) "Turn off iCloud Sync in Settings or sign out of iCloud entirely — either action removes your saved recipes from your iCloud space"** is the user-actionable data-removal documentation 5.1.1(v) wants users to be able to find. **(5) "The app continues to work fully without iCloud sync"** is the AC-41.1 / 5.1.1(ii) contract bullet, restated in user-facing terms. **What we do NOT say.** No promise about CloudKit's encryption posture (Apple's responsibility, not ours — they can change it; we shouldn't make a claim that depends on Apple's continued behavior). No promise about "uptime" or "availability" of the sync (network-dependent; we shouldn't promise what we don't control). No mention of the four analytics events (those live under the existing Usage Data paragraph already in the policy — they don't get their own paragraph because they're not user-identifiable data, they're product-interaction telemetry).

  **The TestFlight gate enforcement.** Per AC-41.12, the build that turns on CloudKit sync MUST NOT submit to TestFlight before this paragraph is live on the policy URL. T-700 (this spec PR) captures the paragraph text; the actual paste-into-WordPress operation is ned's editorial step. The TestFlight pre-flight checklist in `Marketing/TestFlight.md` is updated by this PR with a new gate item: "Verify dutchovendaddy.com/app-privacy/ contains the CL-95 'Optional iCloud Sync' paragraph before submitting any build that enables CloudKit sync." The check is a one-time before the build hits TestFlight, then the gate is satisfied for all subsequent builds (until the paragraph is removed or US-41 is itself reverted).

  **Why ship the paragraph in the spec PR even though the operational web update is later.** Three reasons. **(1) The exact copy needs review.** Spencer + ned need a chance to read + edit before paste. Shipping it in T-700 means review happens in PR conversation, not in a chat thread. **(2) The TestFlight gate needs documentation somewhere.** Putting the gate text + the paragraph in `Marketing/AppPrivacy.md` + `Marketing/TestFlight.md` means future contributors can read the docs + know what the policy update is for. **(3) The "spec must define what privacy says" rule.** Constitution §9's "App Privacy questionnaire must match this section" implies the privacy policy URL content is also a spec-governed artifact (we don't get to silently change the policy text on the website without updating the spec); shipping the paragraph in the spec PR puts the policy text under the same review process as the rest of the spec.

  **Spec/trace:** US-41 / AC-41.12 (the policy update + TestFlight gate); CL-94 (the data-collection framing); CC-6 (App Privacy label). Implementing PRs: T-700 (this spec amendment — also edits `Marketing/AppPrivacy.md` + `Marketing/TestFlight.md`).

- **CL-96 (US-41 / T-707, 2026-05-28)** — **Four new analytics events (`syncEnabled`, `syncDisabled`, `syncCompletedSuccessfully`, `syncFailed(errorCategory:)`); constitution §9 allowlist amended; payloads carry no PII; the `syncFailed` `errorCategory` is a closed enum drawn from a fixed five-value set.** AC-41.9 specifies the event names + payloads; this CL records the rationale + the closed-enum posture that keeps the new events allowlist-safe.

  **Event definitions.**

  | Event | Payload | When |
  |---|---|---|
  | `syncEnabled` | empty | User turns sync on (AC-41.2 opt-in primary tap, or AC-41.3 toggle on) |
  | `syncDisabled` | empty | User turns sync off (AC-41.3 toggle off, confirmation alert "Turn off" tap) |
  | `syncCompletedSuccessfully` | empty | At least one successful CloudKit round-trip completed; debounced to fire at most once per 60 seconds |
  | `syncFailed(errorCategory:)` | `errorCategory: SyncErrorCategory` | Retry-budget exhausted (3 consecutive retries failed per AC-41.6) |

  **The `SyncErrorCategory` closed enum.**

  ```swift
  public enum SyncErrorCategory: String, CaseIterable, Sendable {
      case network            // CKError.networkUnavailable / .networkFailure
      case accountStatus      // CKError.notAuthenticated / .accountTemporarilyUnavailable
      case quotaExceeded      // CKError.quotaExceeded
      case serverInternal     // CKError.serverInternalError / .serverRejectedRequest
      case other              // Catch-all for the long tail
  }
  ```

  **Why a closed enum and not the raw `CKError.Code`.** `CKError.Code` has ~30 cases — not all of which are actionable signal to us, and the long tail (especially the "wrap a system error" ones like `.zoneBusy`, `.changeTokenExpired`, etc.) doesn't help dad understand why sync is failing for users in the field. The closed five-value enum collapses the long tail into one bucket (`other`) and keeps the dashboard signal interpretable. The closed-set posture matches the constitution §9 allowlist convention — closed enums (like `widgetOpened.kind` ("featured" / "saved"), `voiceCommandFired.command` ("next" / "previous" / "repeat" / "pause"), `voiceModeToggled.on` (bool)) are allowed because the wire payload can never carry user-derived content. **No `CKError` message string in the payload.** Even though `CKError.localizedDescription` would be useful for debugging, it's a Foundation-localized string that can vary by locale and is not bounded by an enum — that's a long tail of payload values that the allowlist closed-enum convention exists to prevent. Logging the full `CKError` chain is done via the existing `Logger` plumbing (per AC-41.7) for support purposes; it never leaves the device.

  **Why no recipe id in any of the four payloads.** Sync is a *session-level* state, not a per-recipe action. The existing `recipeSaved` event already captures per-recipe save granularity. Adding `recipeID` to `syncCompletedSuccessfully` would either (a) fire one event per synced row (double-counting against `recipeSaved` and creating a TelemetryDeck event-count explosion) or (b) emit only one event per sync round-trip with an arbitrary recipeID, which would be misleading (the event represents the whole round-trip, not one recipe). The decision in CL-96 sub-table: no per-recipe granularity on the sync events; rely on the existing `recipeSaved` event for that signal.

  **Constitution §9 amendment paragraph (lands paired with T-707).** The "Events tracked are limited to:" sentence in §9 gets four new event-name entries (matching the prior `widgetOpened` / `voiceModeToggled` / `voiceCommandFired` amendment-history pattern): `syncEnabled` (empty payload), `syncDisabled` (empty payload), `syncCompletedSuccessfully` (empty payload), `syncFailed(errorCategory:)` (closed-set `SyncErrorCategory` enum string). The App Privacy questionnaire mapping table is **unchanged** per CL-94 — all four events fall under the existing "Usage Data — Product Interaction" row. Dating + lineage marker: the §9 paragraph entry is dated 2026-05-28 + cites T-707 + CL-96, matching the existing dating convention for amendments (e.g., the `widgetOpened` entry is dated 2026-05-24 / US-17 widget-cluster amendment).

  **Dashboards.** With the four events on TelemetryDeck, dad gets visibility into: (a) opt-in rate ("of users who saw the AC-41.2 prompt, what % tapped Turn On"), (b) sustained adoption ("what % of opted-in users still have sync enabled after 30 days"), (c) success rate ("of sync round-trips, what % succeed"), (d) failure modes ("of failures, what's the distribution across the five `errorCategory` buckets"). All four signals are device-state aggregates, no per-user tracking, no recipe-specific data.

  **Considered alternatives.** **(a) Track `syncStarted` + `syncEnded` instead of `syncCompletedSuccessfully`** — rejected because the start/end pair would emit per-round-trip (every save fires one round-trip), causing event-count inflation; the single-debounced-success event captures the same "is sync working" signal at 1/60th the event volume. **(b) Include the synced row count in `syncCompletedSuccessfully`** — rejected because row-count would be a free-form integer payload (not allowlist-safe) and the per-row granularity already lives in `recipeSaved`. **(c) Drop the `errorCategory` field entirely (emit `syncFailed` with empty payload)** — rejected because the distribution-across-buckets is the most actionable signal for dad ("are most failures network or quota? that's where I focus"); empty payload loses that. **(d) Add a `syncQuotaWarning` event** — deferred to a v1.x follow-up if CloudKit quota actually becomes a problem; the long-tail-of-events rule per constitution §9 says don't add events speculatively.

  **Spec/trace:** US-41 / AC-41.9 (the events); constitution §9 (the amendment); CC-5 (analytics scope). Implementing PRs: T-700 (this spec amendment) + T-707 (the events + §9 amendment).

- **CL-97 (US-41 / T-702 + T-704, 2026-05-28)** — **Backward compatibility: existing v1.0 users keep working unchanged; migration is purely additive; the rollback story is also clean.** AC-41.11 specifies the contract; this CL records the specific compatibility surfaces + the rollback path.

  **Pre-upgrade state.** Existing v1.0 users (anyone running a build before T-702 ships) have: (a) a SwiftData store at the existing SchemaV3 (or SchemaV4 if T-682 has shipped by the time T-702 lands), (b) Keychain entries for guest identity if they've posted comments/ratings, (c) UserDefaults entries for Search recents + Settings preferences + the onboarding flag, (d) the Live Activity / Cook Mode / Voice Mode state which is per-foreground-session (not persisted), (e) home-screen + lock-screen widgets configured.

  **What T-702 adds to the SwiftData store.** One new field on `CachedRecipe` (`modifiedAt: Date?` — additive Optional column, lightweight migration per the established R-5 rule in `Packages/DODPersistence/MIGRATION.md`). No schema bump (SchemaV3 stays the version — the in-place additive-optional-column trick the T-640 article-body column uses applies here too per the SchemaV4.swift comment block). **What T-702 adds to UserDefaults.** Two new flags: `dod.cloudKitSyncOptInPromptShownV1` (bool, default `false`) and `dod.cloudKitSyncEnabled` (bool, default `false`). Both flags default `false`, so a never-launched-with-sync user is in the "never prompted, not enabled" state — exactly what AC-41.2 expects. **What T-702 adds to entitlements.** The two iCloud entitlement keys from CL-93 (`com.apple.developer.icloud-container-identifiers` + `com.apple.developer.icloud-services`). The existing `com.apple.security.application-groups` key is untouched.

  **The first cold launch after upgrade.** AC-41.2's opt-in sheet appears (gated by the `dod.cloudKitSyncOptInPromptShownV1 = false` state). The user has three paths: **(a) Tap "Turn on iCloud Sync"** → flips both flags to true, begins first sync round-trip (the local SwiftData store's saved recipes upload to CloudKit, eventually mirroring on other devices on the same Apple ID); **(b) Tap "Not now"** → flips `dod.cloudKitSyncOptInPromptShownV1 = true` only; `dod.cloudKitSyncEnabled` stays false; the app behaves identically to the pre-upgrade build (no CloudKit activity); **(c) Dismiss without tapping** (swipe down on the sheet) → identical to (b); the `Not now` is the default decision when the user dismisses without acting.

  **Existing saved recipes.** Path (a) above uploads them to CloudKit; path (b) or (c) leaves them as-is. No data loss in any path. The bridge from "local-only saved" → "synced saved" is a one-way transition the user initiates; the local data is preserved through it.

  **Rollback story.** If a critical bug forces us to ship a future build that disables CloudKit sync entirely (e.g., a P0 sync-data-loss bug), the rollback path is clean: the build removes the SwiftData adapter's `cloudKitDatabase:` parameter (or hard-codes it to nil), leaving the local SwiftData store intact, the `dod.cloudKitSyncEnabled` UserDefaults flag becomes a no-op (read but not acted on), the Settings → iCloud Sync row disappears (the conditional `#if` or feature flag the row is wrapped in renders it absent), the CloudKit-side data is **NOT** deleted (the user's saves are still in their iCloud private DB and could be re-mirrored if a future build re-enables sync). The user keeps their local saves, sees the Settings row vanish, and loses cross-device consistency for any saves made in the rollback window — but no data is lost on either side. **Why this rollback story is "clean."** It's clean because the CloudKit-side data persists independently of the app's use of it — `iCloud.com.dutchovendaddy.DODApp` records survive on Apple's servers under the user's Apple ID for as long as the user has iCloud, regardless of what our app does with them. Re-enabling sync in a future build naturally re-hydrates from those records. The user loses nothing in a rollback; they lose only sync during the rollback window. **Considered alternatives we rejected for rollback.** Deleting CloudKit data on rollback — rejected because if the rollback is reversed (we re-enable sync in a subsequent build), the user has to re-upload everything from scratch; preserving the CloudKit data lets us reverse the rollback gracefully.

  **AC-5.7 amendment.** The existing AC-5.7 limitation ("reinstall wipes saves") is amended in the same PR cluster: with sync enabled, a fresh install on the same Apple ID restores the user's saved recipes via the AC-41.2 opt-in flow's first sync round-trip. The amendment is via a strike-through + lineage marker in spec.md per the established convention (CL-38's heart-to-bookmark amendment, CL-9's blocklist amendment).

  **Spec/trace:** US-41 / AC-41.11 (the contract); AC-5.7 (the amended limitation); CL-86 (the parent CloudKit decision); MIGRATION.md R-5 (the additive-only schema rule). Implementing PRs: T-700 (this spec amendment) + T-702 (the additive migration + UserDefaults flags).

- **CL-98 (US-41 / T-700, 2026-05-28)** — **Google Play / Android non-applicability deferral: no Android target exists; this CL is the documented "what would change" trail for a future port.** Constitution §2 explicitly scopes the app to iPhone + iPad (universal) with no watchOS / macOS / visionOS. There is no Android target. The round-6 backlog entry asked for "Google + Apple + email login" — the Google sign-in piece would naturally support an eventual Android port (because Google sign-in is the Android-native identity surface), but with the CloudKit pivot per CL-86, the iOS surface no longer needs Google sign-in for its own use case. **Decision: Google Play compliance is non-applicable for v1.x. Document the re-evaluation trigger and what would change if Android is ever added.**

  **Re-evaluation trigger.** If/when constitution §2 is amended to add an Android target (a v2-or-later scope decision; not part of v1.x), the spec needs to graduate a new US for Android-side sync identity. CloudKit private DB is iOS-only and is not available on Android — the Android port cannot use CloudKit for cross-device sync. The natural Android paths are: **(a) Firebase Auth + Firestore** — the Google-native BaaS combo, supports Google sign-in + Apple sign-in (via Firebase Auth's Apple provider), syncs structured documents; **(b) Supabase Auth + Postgres** — open-source Firebase alternative, similar feature surface; **(c) Custom server + OAuth** — the original Spencer round-6 framing, now applicable to the Android case rather than the iOS case.

  **The iOS spec would split when Android lands.** US-41's CloudKit posture stays exclusively for the iOS target; the Android target's spec graduates a new US (US-XX, slot reserved post-Android-graduation) for the Android-side sync identity. The two targets share *no* common sync backend in v1.x — the iOS user's saves live in their iCloud DB; an eventual Android user's saves would live in Firebase / Supabase / our custom backend; cross-platform-same-user-account sync (a user with both an iPhone and an Android device wanting their saves to merge) is **not** a v1.x or v2 goal — it would be a v3+ "unified backend" spec that bridges both platforms.

  **Other "Google Play / Android" non-applicabilities for completeness.** **(1) Google Play data-safety form.** N/A — no Android target. **(2) Android-specific consent flows** (the Android equivalent of ATT, currently the Google Play Data Safety form). N/A. **(3) Google Play store-listing privacy URL** — when Android lands, this would point at the same `dutchovendaddy.com/app-privacy/` URL but with platform-specific text added for the Firebase/Supabase posture. **(4) Cross-app advertising identifiers** (the Android Advertising ID equivalent of the IDFA). N/A — no Android target. **(5) Google Play family policy** (if the app were ever for kids). N/A — we have no kids designation on iOS either.

  **Why ship this CL even though there's no Android target.** Two reasons. **(1) The round-6 Spencer entry explicitly listed "Google" as one of the three OAuth providers.** A future reader of the round-6 entry deserves to see the trail to "why didn't this happen" — CL-98 is that trail. **(2) The future-Android-port question will come up.** Dad or Spencer will, eventually, ask "should we ship for Android?" — having the CL already documented (instead of needing to re-derive the analysis when the question arises) saves the conversation. The CL also names the natural Android backend paths so a future graduation can start from a known-considered position.

  **Spec/trace:** US-41 / AC-41.1 (iOS-only contract); constitution §2 (the iOS-only platform scope); CL-86 (the CloudKit decision that makes Google sign-in non-applicable for iOS); CL-87 (the OAuth reversal trail). Implementing PRs: T-700 (this spec amendment — pure documentation).

- **CL-99 (US-41 / T-702 + T-705, 2026-05-28)** — **CloudKit subscription strategy: accept SwiftData's default `CKDatabaseSubscription` posture (silent push triggers a sync wakeup); do NOT add custom record-zone subscriptions or per-record predicate subscriptions in v1.x.** SwiftData's CloudKit adapter creates a default `CKDatabaseSubscription` on first sync that registers the app for silent push notifications on any change to the private DB. When the user saves a recipe on Device A, Apple's CloudKit infra fires a silent push to Device B, which wakes up the background sync process on Device B and pulls the change. This is the "your iPad updates within seconds of saving on iPhone" UX path. **Decision: accept the default subscription posture; document what we're getting + what we're not adding.**

  **What we get with the default.** **(1) Silent push wakeups on private-DB changes.** Apple's CloudKit fires a push notification with `aps: { content-available: 1 }` when the user's private DB changes; iOS routes it to the app's background mode (specifically `remote-notification`, which SwiftData's adapter requests via its built-in entitlement); the adapter handles the wakeup, fetches the changeset, applies it locally. **(2) Automatic subscription creation.** No `CKModifySubscriptionsOperation` code on our side; SwiftData handles it. **(3) Subscription identity persistence across launches.** Each subscription gets a `subscriptionID` stored in SwiftData's internal metadata; no churn / re-creation on relaunch.

  **What we don't add (the considered features we deliberately skip).** **(a) Custom record-zone subscriptions per-recipe.** Could be useful for ranking sync priority (e.g., "saved-yesterday-recipe gets pushed faster than saved-3-months-ago"), but we don't need it — CloudKit's default fan-out is fast enough for our scale. **(b) Predicate subscriptions** (e.g., "only push me records matching X"). Don't need — every record in our scope is meant to sync. **(c) Long-polling fallback.** Don't need — the silent-push path is iOS 10+ standard, and devices that miss a push (e.g., offline for hours) catch up on next foreground via the standard sync round-trip.

  **The `remote-notification` background mode entitlement.** SwiftData's CloudKit adapter automatically requires the `remote-notification` UIBackgroundModes value in Info.plist. T-701's scope adds this key to `App/Info.plist` paired with the CL-93 entitlements work (it's a `project.yml`-managed key — the entitlements file change is one half; the Info.plist key is the other half). **Why this isn't a new "background fetch" surface that contradicts NFR-3 ("background refresh disabled by default").** NFR-3 governs *content fetching* in the background (e.g., pre-fetching new recipes from the WP REST API in the background, which we explicitly don't do). The CloudKit silent-push path is a *sync* surface — it pulls the user's own data changes from the user's own iCloud space, not new content from the blog. The NFR-3 "no surprise data use" intent is preserved because the only data flowing is what the user already authored on another of their own devices.

  **TelemetryDeck visibility into the subscription path.** When a silent push wakes the app, the `syncCompletedSuccessfully` event (CL-96) fires per the standard sync-completion path. The push notification itself is not telemetered (it's a system-routed signal, not a user action). This keeps the analytics surface bounded to the four events in CL-96; no `silentPushReceived` or `backgroundSyncStarted` events are added.

  **Spec/trace:** US-41 / AC-41.6 (offline behavior — silent push is one of the wakeup paths); constitution NFR-3 (background refresh disabled — sync is not "fetch new content," it's "pull user's own changes"). Implementing PRs: T-700 (this spec amendment, which documents the subscription posture) + T-701 (the entitlements + Info.plist key) + T-702 (the sync adapter that accepts the default subscription) + T-705 (the status surface that observes the sync state changes the silent push triggers).

---

**Phase 15 summary.** Fourteen CLs (CL-86 through CL-99, with CL-84 skipped per the prior-phase parallel renumbering) resolve the architectural pivot Spencer's round-6 capture paused on. CL-86 carries the load-bearing decision (CloudKit private DB over WordPress users / Firebase-Supabase / local-only + manual iCloud sync). CL-87 records the OAuth-reversal trail (no Account icon, no login sheet, no providers; the user's iCloud account is the sync identity). CL-88 enumerates exactly what syncs (saved recipes only, with `PersistentShoppingListItem` deferred until T-682 ships). CL-89 captures the opt-in flow. CL-90 the conflict-resolution rule. CL-91 the offline + status posture. CL-92 the "account deletion" semantics that satisfy App Store 5.1.1(v) without an explicit Delete button. CL-93 the entitlements + container provisioning. CL-94 the App Privacy nutrition-label delta (no new category, per Apple's CloudKit-specific guidance). CL-95 the privacy-policy paragraph. CL-96 the four new analytics events + §9 amendment. CL-97 the backward-compat + rollback story. CL-98 the Google Play / Android non-applicability deferral. CL-99 the CloudKit subscription posture. **Two explicit deferrals:** (i) CL-88's sub-decision that `PersistentShoppingListItem` syncing waits until T-682 lands the SwiftData @Model; (ii) CL-98's Android non-applicability deferral. **Constitution amendments** (paired with T-707 per the existing convention): §1 (v2 sync model subsection), §3 (CloudKit.framework Apple-provided), §4 (Synced records data boundary), §9 (four new events). The implementation plan is T-700 (this spec PR) + T-701 (entitlements + container) + T-702 (SwiftData → CloudKit adapter) + T-703 (Settings toggle) + T-704 (opt-in sheet) + T-705 (offline queue + status surface) + T-706 (conflict-resolution + tests) + T-707 (analytics + §9 amendment) + T-708 (L1 + L3 + L5 tests). **Coordination:** all round-8 parallels are landed (T-660 / T-670 / T-680..T-680c / T-690a..T-690c shipped per the backlog Recently-graduated trail through US-39 + US-40). T-700 reserves CL-86..CL-99 (CL-84 skipped), US-41, T-700..T-708. Spec-file collisions on `clarifications.md` / `spec.md` / `tasks.md` / `backlog.md` are expected at merge time and the rebaser picks the next free CL/US/T slot — no parallel round-9 work is currently in-flight, so this PR lands cleanly.
